ABC News Live Video Local Shows Good Morning America ShopGMA Interest Successfully Added We'll notify you here with news about Turn on desktop notifications for breaking stories about interest? OffOn Stream on Officials in Arizona, Georgia monitoring some hantavirus cruise ship passengers who have returned to US None of the residents being monitored in the U.S. have shown signs of illness. ByZoe Magee, Dragana Jovanovic, and Mary Kekatos May 7, 2026, 3:13 AM Share The total number of confirmed hantavirus cases aboard a cruise ship has risen to five as global health authorities work to contain a potentially deadly cluster of the disease. More than 100 passengers remain on the ship, MV Hondius, and the World Health Organization (WHO) is monitoring their health. Officials said that the "overall public health risk remains low" but that there may be some person-to-person spread. In its first public statement on the outbreak on Wednesday evening, the U.S. Centers for Disease Control and Prevention said, "the risk to the American public is extremely low" and urged Americans aboard the ship to follow the guidance of health officials. "Our top priority remains the health and safety of all U.S. passengers," the CDC statement said. "The Department of State is leading a coordinated, whole-of-government response including direct contact with passengers, diplomatic coordination, and engagement with domestic and international health authorities." Officials in Arizona, Georgia monitoring passengers who have returned to US Health officials in multiple states in the U.S. say they are monitoring some passengers who have returned to the U.S. after being aboard the ship for potential hantavirus infections. The Arizona Department of Health Services said it is monitoring one resident that was a passenger on the ship. The Georgia Department of Public Health said it is monitoring two residents who returned home after disembarking from the Hondius. None of the former passengers being monitored in the U.S. have shown any signs of illness, officials said. Cape Verdean police officers stand guard as crew members of an ambulance boat getting in hazmat suites preparing to head to the cruise ship MV Hondius, while stationary off the port of Praia, the capital of Cape Verde, on May 6, 2026. AFP via Getty Images Ship's route The ship, which was off the coast of Africa in Cape Verde, is now en route to the Canary Islands after officials medically evacuated three people, including two in "serious condition." The trip is expected to take three to four days, the ship's operator, Oceanwide Expeditions, said Wednesday. Some passengers disembarked the ship before knowledge of the cluster and are back in their home countries. In some cases, authorities are advising those passengers to self-isolate. The two patients who were evacuated have landed in the Netherlands and "have been received by specialist medical and screening teams," Oceanwide Expeditions said Wednesday. In addition to those two, a third person, who is asymptomatic but a close contact of a German national who died on May 2, was also removed from the ship, WHO officials in Cape Verde told ABC News. The medical aircraft transporting the individual is experiencing a delay, Oceanwide Expeditions said Wednesday. "WHO continues to work with the ship’s operators to closely monitor the health of passengers and crew, working with countries to support appropriate medical follow-up and evacuation where needed," Dr. Tedros Adhanom Ghebreyesus, the director-general of the WHO, said in a post on X on Wednesday, in which he confirmed the evacuations. "Monitoring and follow-up for passengers on board and for those who have already disembarked has been initiated in collaboration with the ship's operators and national health authorities," he continued. Tedros added that "the overall public health risk remains low." For now, there are no symptomatic passengers who remain on board the ship, Dr. Freddy Banza, a WHO doctor who helped evacuate the three people, told reporters Wednesday. The people now considered at highest risk are the crew members and medical staff who spent the most time caring for sick passengers, he said. Related 3 people waiting to be medically evacuated from cruise ship at center of suspected hantavirus outbreak Health officials confirmed two additional cases of hantavirus among crewmembers, bringing the total confirmed cases to five. "He had responded to an email from the ship’s operator informing the passengers of the health event, and presented himself to a hospital in Zurich, Switzerland, and is receiving care." 3 deaths recorded The three previously confirmed hantavirus cases include a woman who disembarked and was on her way home from the Netherlands, a British national who is in critical but stable condition in a hospital in Johannesburg, and a passenger who traveled on the first leg of the voyage and is currently being treated at the University Hospital Zurich, according to Oceanwide Expeditions. So far, three deaths have been recorded. In a statement, the Dutch airline KLM said it had been informed by Dutch health authorities that one of the people who died from the hantavirus was briefly aboard a KLM aircraft in Johannesburg, South Africa, on April 25. "Due to the passenger’s medical condition at the time, the crew decided not to allow the passenger to travel on the flight," the airline said in the statement. "The passenger sadly later passed away in Johannesburg." The airline said all passengers who were on board the flight were being notified. The type of virus in this outbreak has been confirmed as Andes hantavirus by the National Institute for Communicable Diseases in South Africa and Geneva University Hospitals in Switzerland, the WHO said Wednesday. The Andes hantavirus historically has been shown to potentially transmit between people, according to the WHO. Oceanwide Expeditions said of the three passengers who were evacuated from the ship, two are symptomatic and in serious condition and the third is asymptomatic but a close contact of a German national who died on May 2. This aerial view shows crew members wearing hazmat suits on board of a boat heading towards the port from the cruise ship MV Hondius, stationary off the port of Praia, the capital of Cape Verde, on May 6, 2026. AFP via Getty Images "In partnership with the RIVM (Dutch Institute for Public Health and Environment), Oceanwide Expeditions is expanding medical care on board with two infectious disease physicians, arriving today by plane from the Netherlands. This ensures that optimal medical care can be provided if necessary, during the next stage of this evolving situation," the company said in a statement. Cape Verde officials said on Tuesday that the vessel was expected to sail to the Spanish island of Tenerife, but the president of the Canary Islands, a Spanish archipelago off the northwestern coast of Africa, said on Wednesday that the regional government was opposed to allowing the luxury cruise ship to dock in Tenerife. "This decision is not based on any technical criteria, nor is there sufficient information to reassure the public or guarantee their safety," President Fernando Clavijo told radio station COPE, according to Reuters. Related American speaks out from cruise ship amid deadly hantavirus outbreak Clavijo said on social media that he had requested a meeting with the Spanish prime minister to discuss the ship. He added that the Canary Islands "always acts with responsibility, but it cannot accept decisions taken behind the backs of the Canary Islands institutions and without sufficient information to the population." Mónica García, Spain's minister of health, said once the ship arrives at the port of Granadilla de Abona in the Canary Islands, there will be a "joint screening and evacuation mechanism will be launched to repatriate all passengers," according to RTVE, a Spanish national public broadcaster. A general view of the cruise ship MV Hondius, while stationary off the port of Praia, the capital of Cape Verde, on May 6, 2026. AFP via Getty Images “Unless their medical condition prevents it, all foreign passengers will be repatriated through the European civil protection mechanism, about which the Interior Minister will provide further details later," Garcia said in Spanish. WHO officials earlier on Wednesday said the three evacuated people were to be transferred to planes bound for both the Netherlands and Tenerife, but later updated the plan so that all would be sent to the Netherlands, officials told ABC News. ABC News' Othon Leyva contributed to this report. Icahn School of Medicine at Mount Sinai Reaches Top 10 as a National Institutes of Health-Funded Academic Medical Center | Newswise - Newswise

Icahn School of Medicine at Mount Sinai Reaches Top 10 as a National Institutes of Health-Funded Academic Medical Center | Newswise - Newswise
新闻简报The Icahn School of Medicine at Mount Sinai has achieved a significant milestone by ranking No. 9 nationally in biomedical research grant funding from the National Institutes of Health (NIH) in fiscal year 2026. This accomplishment is highlighted by its position as No. 5 in North America and No. 6 globally for healthcare research in the 2026 Nature Index Research Leaders Rankings. The school's research spans diverse fields such as brain disorders, immunology, cancer, heart disease, genomics, AI, and pandemic preparedness, with over $514 million in total NIH funding across 696 grants and contracts. The achievements reflect the institution's commitment to scientific discovery and innovation, as well as its unique model of integrating research and clinical care. Mount Sinai is also leading a pivotal multi-site randomized control trial of deep brain stimulation for depression, showcasing its ongoing efforts in advancing medical treatments. These developments underscore the school's sustained investment in biomedical research and its dedication to improving patient care and health equity.

Icahn School of Medicine at Mount Sinai Reaches Top 10 as a National Institutes of Health-Funded Academic Medical Center | Newswise - Newswise

News Brief
The Icahn School of Medicine at Mount Sinai has achieved a significant milestone by ranking No. 9 nationally in biomedical research grant funding from the National Institutes of Health (NIH) in fiscal year 2026. This accomplishment is highlighted by its position as No. 5 in North America and No. 6 globally for healthcare research in the 2026 Nature Index Research Leaders Rankings. The school's research spans diverse fields such as brain disorders, immunology, cancer, heart disease, genomics, AI, and pandemic preparedness, with over $514 million in total NIH funding across 696 grants and contracts. The achievements reflect the institution's commitment to scientific discovery and innovation, as well as its unique model of integrating research and clinical care. Mount Sinai is also leading a pivotal multi-site randomized control trial of deep brain stimulation for depression, showcasing its ongoing efforts in advancing medical treatments. These developments underscore the school's sustained investment in biomedical research and its dedication to improving patient care and health equity.
Original source text (English)
Icahn School of Medicine at Mount Sinai Reaches Top 10 as a National Institutes of Health-Funded Academic Medical Center A different model for medical school and a bold approach to biomedical research Newswise — It was a bold, even audacious, idea—establish a new medical school without the traditional moorings of a university, the entrenched model for nearly every academic medical center in the country. Since its founding in 1968, the Icahn School of Medicine at Mount Sinai, as it was renamed in 2013, has become a leading research enterprise with an integrated science - plus - medicine formula for success in the pursuit of life-saving discoveries. “This has been a milestone year for Mount Sinai’s research and education enterprises. From our humble beginnings in a vacant bus garage in upper Manhattan to becoming a life-sciences and educational powerhouse, we have propelled science and medicine thanks in part to our passion and grit,” says Eric J. Nestler, MD, PhD, Anne and Joel Ehrenkranz Dean, Icahn School of Medicine at Mount Sinai. In fiscal year 2026, which ended September 30, the School advanced for the first time into the top 10 medical schools nationally in research grants from the NIH. Ranked No. 5 in North America and No. 6 globally for health care research in the 2026 Nature Index Research Leaders Rankings, Mount Sinai was also recognized this year by the Blue Ridge Institute for Medical Research for 13 of its specialties making the top 10 nationwide for National Institutes of Health (NIH) funding, a reflection of awards garnered in 2025. "We are proud to be one of the nation’s top 10 recipients of NIH funding. The Icahn School of Medicine at Mount Sinai is a free-standing medical school—the only one to stand alongside the country’s leading universities. This accomplishment reflects the exceptional talent and innovation that defines Mount Sinai,” said Brendan G. Carr, MD, MA, MS, Chief Executive Officer, Mount Sinai Health System . "Our unique model brings researchers and clinicians together, allowing discoveries in the lab to move more quickly to the patients and communities who need them most. We are proud to be a leading voice in shaping the future of medicine.” There is no better evidence of the steady growth and momentum it has achieved than this new milestone: placing No. 9 nationally in biomedical research grant funding from the NIH. The Icahn School of Medicine now ranks higher than any other medical school in New York State in NIH-funded biomedical research and has the distinction of being the only freestanding medical school among the top 10 NIH grant recipients. In fiscal year 2026, Mount Sinai investigators secured more than $514 million in total NIH funding across 696 grants and contracts in areas as diverse as the brain, immunology, cancer, heart, genomics, AI and human health, and vaccines and pandemic preparedness. Mount Sinai’s educational efforts are closely aligned with its research and clinical enterprises. The Icahn School of Medicine, Graduate School of Biomedical Sciences, and Phillips School of Nursing are training the next generations of physicians, scientists, nurses, and public health and administrative practitioners. “We were determined nearly 60 years ago to break the mold in biomedical research and education by creating a school within a hospital, one that could build on the hospital’s legacy of state-of-the-art and compassionate care for a unique patient population,” says Dr. Nestler. “Reaching these milestones in a singular year is a testament to our resolve and boldness. Our investigators share a collective ethos—a commitment to scientific discovery, a willingness to tackle difficult questions, and a relentless pursuit of new knowledge—and their north star is making discoveries that become life-saving therapies that change peoples’ lives. That is the ultimate achievement.” In a field where progress has been notoriously slow, few research teams have dug deeper than Mount Sinai’s into the complex cellular architecture of brain disorders that are among the leading causes of disability worldwide, like Alzheimer’s disease and Parkinson’s disease. Mount Sinai investigators, led by Panos Roussos, MD, PhD, have played leading roles in the global network known as the PsychAD Consortium, which has produced a landmark collection of nine papers enhancing the field’s understanding of why Alzheimer’s disease, for one, progresses differently among individuals. The work offers new insights that may inform future precision medicine strategies. Mount Sinai has also been a pioneer, under Helen Mayberg, MD’s and Brian Kopell, MD’s leadership, in advancing, through decades of NIH-funded studies, deep brain stimulation (DBS). Mount Sinai’s Living Brain Project, an NIH-funded investigation directed by Alex Charney, MD, PhD, was developed to evaluate DBS for Parkinson’s disease, obsessive-compulsive disorder, essential tremor, dystonia, and depression. The surgical treatment, which, involves implanting electrodes into the brain has been a game-changer for many of these common disorders. Mount Sinai is now leading a pivotal multi-site randomized control trial of the use of DBS for depression. Mount Sinai Tisch Cancer Center’s (TCC) designation in 2025 as a “Comprehensive Cancer Center” by the National Cancer Institute represents the highest designation awarded to cancer centers in the United States. This recognition places Mount Sinai among the top 1 percent of cancer centers nationwide and affirms the breadth and depth of its cancer research, clinical excellence, and commitment to health equity. This milestone, along with Mount Sinai’s top 10 funding in cancer research, underscores our sustained investment in cancer science, commitment to training the next generation of investigators, and a promise to improve cancer care. Mount Sinai oncologists, under the direction of Ramon Parsons, MD, PhD, Luis Isola, MD, and Joseph Sparano, MD, have advanced the field on several fronts, including cancer vaccines, bone marrow transplantation, blood cancers, multiple myeloma, bladder cancer, and other solid tumors. Researchers at TCC have led transformative studies across a wide spectrum of cancers, advancing treatments that are changing standards of care worldwide including pioneering trials resulting in the FDA approval of a therapy to prevent recurrence in patients with muscle-invasive bladder cancer, an innovative vaccine for urothelial cancer and a new vaccine platform for solid and hematologic malignancies. Advances in heart disease, the No. 1 killer of Americans, have been a critical focus for Mount Sinai investigators who have turned their lens to the less-studied populations of women and children. A newly launched NIH-funded study, led by Annetine Gelijns, PhD and Roxana Mehran, MD at Mount Sinai Fuster Heart Hospital, is focused on uncovering the optimal therapy for spontaneous coronary artery dissection, the disease that took the life of U.S. Senator Lindsey Graham in July 2026. This condition disproportionately affects women, often at a young age, without the traditional warning signs of heart disease. Another research grant for $8.5 million is continuing cutting-edge work aimed at improving long-term outcomes for children with congenital heart disease, while a $10.2 million award is bringing the institution closer to identifying the risk factors for malignant ventricular arrhythmias in mitral valve prolapse. Mount Sinai proudly applauded the appointment in March 2026 of Dr. Mehran, Mount Sinai Professor in Cardiovascular Clinical Research and Outcomes, as the 74 th President of the American College of Cardiology. Autoimmune diseases like rheumatoid arthritis, type 1 diabetes, inflammatory bowel disease, and psoriasis are becoming increasingly common in developed nations. Autoimmune diseases affect more than 15 million Americans across more than 105 conditions that can damage nearly every organ system. Mount Sinai researchers, led by Miriam Merad, MD, PhD and Brian Brown, PhD, have focused on new discoveries for patients wrestling with the hallmarks of autoimmune disease, including unpredictable cycles of flare and remission, delayed diagnosis, and limited treatment options, by applying cellular science and precision medicine. This year, the institution garnered a new $5 million private grant to establish the Colton Center for Autoimmunity , with Shruti Naik, PhD, as its director. Residing within Mount Sinai’s Precision Immunology Institute (PrIISM), the center will work with international partners to create large-scale datasets across multiple autoimmune diseases and apply AI-enabled analytics to help fundamentally change how autoimmune diseases are detected, treated, and prevented. The rapid spread of viruses like influenza, dengue, hantavirus, and COVID-19 has thrust Mount Sinai into prominent national and global roles focused on disease prevention. Mount Sinai researchers recently received a five-year award of nearly $21 million to advance next-generation technologies against respiratory threats. The project will begin with an H5N1 flu vaccine prototype and development of a vaccine platform framework for broader, safer, and more durable immune protection. This undertaking will include finding a novel way of approaching influenza in search of protection that is more far-reaching, rather than narrowly focused on a single circulating influenza virus variant. During the recent outbreak of hantavirus pulmonary syndrome on a cruise ship resulting in 12 confirmed cases and three deaths, Mount Sinai investigators helped coordinate the international response teams and scientific inquiry that followed. That experience triggered a new $11.8 million federal grant to Mount Sinai to fundamentally change this country’s course of vaccine development. Known as the DOORs project, the research team, led by distinguished virologists Gustavo Palacios, PhD, and Adolfo García-Sastre, PhD in the Department of Microbiology, chaired by Ana Sesma, PhD, is charged with identifying and closing the critical “doors” that whole families of viruses exploit to infect, replicate, and spread. “This approach to pandemic preparedness and vaccine development underlines how we move medicine and science forward, finding new paths and ways of doing things for the good of humankind,” says Dr. Nestler. About the Icahn School of Medicine at Mount Sinai The Icahn School of Medicine at Mount Sinai is internationally renowned for its outstanding research, educational, and clinical care programs. It is the sole academic partner for the seven member hospitals* of the Mount Sinai Health System, one of the largest academic health systems in the United States, providing care to New York City’s large and diverse patient population. The Icahn School of Medicine at Mount Sinai offers highly competitive MD, PhD, MD-PhD, and master’s degree programs, with enrollment of more than 1,200 students. It has the largest graduate medical education program in the country, with more than 2,700 clinical residents and fellows training throughout the Health System. The Graduate School of Biomedical Sciences offers 12 degree-granting programs, conducts innovative basic and translational research, and trains more than 470 postdoctoral research fellows. Ranked No. 9 nationwide in National Institutes of Health (NIH) grant funding, the Icahn School of Medicine at Mount Sinai is among the 90th percentile of U.S. private medical schools in Sponsored Programs Direct Expenditures per Principal Investigator, according to the Association of American Medical Colleges. More than 6,900 scientists, educators, and clinicians work within and across dozens of academic departments and multidisciplinary institutes with an emphasis on translational research and therapeutics. Through Mount Sinai Innovation Partners (MSIP), the Health System facilitates the real-world application and commercialization of medical breakthroughs made at Mount Sinai. Mount Sinai’s Phillips School of Nursing currently enrolls more than 400 students and offers accelerated BSN programs and a variety of innovative curricula responding to the special needs of working adults interested in entering the nursing profession. ------------------------------------------------------- * Mount Sinai Health System member hospitals: The Mount Sinai Hospital; Mount Sinai Brooklyn; Mount Sinai Morningside; Mount Sinai Queens; Mount Sinai South Nassau; Mount Sinai West; and New York Eye and Ear Infirmary of Mount Sinai

Icahn School of Medicine at Mount Sinai Reaches Top 10 as a National Institutes of Health-Funded Academic Medical Center A different model for medical school and a bold approach to biomedical research Newswise — It was a bold, even audacious, idea—establish a new medical school without the traditional moorings of a university, the entrenched model for nearly every academic medical center in the country.
Location and population
United States, New York State, Mount Sinai Health System.
Event
The Icahn School of Medicine at Mount Sinai reached No. 9 nationally in biomedical research grant funding from the NIH in fiscal year 2026, and was ranked No. 5 in North America and No. 6 globally for health care research in the 2026 Nature Index Research Leaders Rankings. This achievement reflects the school's research and education enterprises, which have been recognized for their contributions to biomedical research.
Scale, impact and risk
Mount Sinai investigators secured over $514 million in total NIH funding across 696 grants and contracts in fiscal year 2026. The school's research spans diverse areas including brain disorders, immunology, cancer, heart disease, genomics, AI, and pandemic preparedness. The funding highlights the institution's significant impact on biomedical research and its potential to advance life-saving therapies.
Response, status and uncertainty
The school's achievements are confirmed through its NIH funding rankings and grants. The response status includes ongoing research and clinical trials, such as the multi-site randomized control trial of deep brain stimulation for depression. The information is sourced from official statements and is not under investigation.
